Static Vacuum Spacetimes with Λ < 0 \Lambda<0 as Attractors of the Ricci-Harmonic Flow
Rasmus Jouttijärvi, Klaus Kröncke, Louis Yudowitz

Abstract
We prove dynamical stability and instability theorems for asymptotically hyperbolic static solutions of Einstein’s equation with Λ < 0 \Lambda<0 , viewed as self-similar solutions of the Ricci-harmonic flow. More precisely, we show that static metrics are dynamically stable if and only if a positive mass type theorem holds for nearby metrics. Our key tool is a new variant of the expander entropy for the Ricci-harmonic flow.



1
Introduction

The Ricci flow is a weakly parabolic evolution equation for Riemannian metrics on a manifold, and has been a major tool for solving numerous open conjectures in Riemannian geometry over the past few decades. There are also various extensions and variants of the Ricci flow in the literature. For possible applications in general relativity, Bernhard List introduced in
[
Li
]
the extended Ricci flow system

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}g(t)&=-2\mathrm{Ric}_{g(t)}+2\alpha\cdot d\varphi(t)\otimes d\varphi(t)\\
\partial_{t}\varphi(t)&=-\Delta\varphi(t),\end{cases}

(1.1)

where
\alpha
is a fixed coupling constant. Here, the evolution of the family of Riemannian metrics
g(t)
is coupled to the evolution of a time-dependent family of real-valued functions
\varphi(t)
. This extended system is usually called the
Ricci-harmonic flow
in the literature.
While one of the motivations for studying the Ricci flow is the possible discovery of new Ricci-flat metrics as limits when
t\to\infty
, the Ricci-harmonic flow has been introduced as a possible tool to find new static solutions of Einstein’s vacuum equations. These are solutions to

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\nabla^{2}V=V\mathrm{Ric}_{g}\\
\Delta V=0,\end{cases}

(1.2)

where
g
is a Riemannian metric and
V
is a positive function on a manifold
M
.
We call
(M,g,V)
a
static triple
and
{\left(g,V\right)}
a
static pair
. Recall that (
1.2
) is equivalent to the statement that the static Lorentzian metric
h=-V^{2}dt^{2}+g
is Ricci-flat. Note that positivity of
V
ensures that
h
is well-defined as a Lorentzian metric. By the substitution
\varphi=-\ln{V}
, one sees that a static triple is a self-similar solution of the Ricci-harmonic flow (
1.1
).
A further generalisation, where the Ricci flow is coupled to the harmonic map heat flow for manifold-valued functions, has been introduced by Müller in
[
Mu
]
. Our attention, however, will be focused on the case of real-valued functions.

In this paper, we will study a normalised variant of the Ricci-harmonic flow which is given by

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}g(t)&=-2{\left(\mathrm{Ric}_{g(t)}+ng(t)-d\varphi(t)\otimes d\varphi(t)\right)}\\
\partial_{t}\varphi(t)&=-{\left(\Delta\varphi(t)-n\right)},\end{cases}

(1.3)

where
n
is the dimension of the underlying manifold. Note that the flows (
1.1
) and (
1.3
) are homothetically equivalent to each other. That is, solutions of these two equations can be related to each other by suitable rescaling of the components and the time variable.

Our motivation to study (
1.3
) is finding static solutions of
Einstein’s equations
with cosmological constant
\Lambda<0
. By imposing the normalisation
\Lambda=-n
, it is given by

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\nabla^{2}V={\left(\mathrm{Ric}_{g}+ng\right)}V\\
\Delta V=-nV,\end{cases}

(1.4)

where the function
V
is again required to be positive. These equations are equivalent to demanding
\mathrm{Ric}_{h}=-nh
for the static Lorentzian metric
h=-V^{2}dt^{2}+g
.
Similarly to the previous case, one can check by substituting
\varphi=-\ln{V}
that a static triple
(M,g,V)
with cosmological constant
\Lambda=-n
is a self-similar solution of the normalised Ricci-harmonic flow (
1.3
). See the discussion in Section
2.2
for more details and perspectives.

The static equations (
1.4
) with
\Lambda=0
are quite rigid. A classic result of Bunting and Masood-ul-Alam (
[
BM
]
) states that Euclidean space together with a constant positive function is the only solution of (
1.4
) such that
(M,g)
is a complete asymptotically flat manifold without boundary. If one allows a boundary, the only other solution is the Schwarzschild family of black holes.
On the other hand, the solution space for
\Lambda<0
exhibits a much richer structure. In
[
ACD
]
, Anderson, Chruściel, and Delay constructed an infinite-dimensional family of nontrivial asymptotically hyperbolic static triples with
\Lambda=-n
.

The purpose of this article is to determine under which conditions such static triples are dynamically stable as self-similar solutions of (
1.3
).
In doing so, we will reveal an interesting and deep connection to the positive mass theorem. Such a relation was conjectured by Ilmanen for the ADM-mass in the ALE setting and partly confirmed in
[
HHS
]
. See also
[
DO
]
. In their recent work
[
KY
]
, the second and third authors showed that dynamical stability of Poincaré-Einstein manifolds is equivalent to a local positive mass theorem involving a new mass-like invariant introduced by Dahl, McCormick and the second author in
[
DKM
]
. The present article is inspired by these results.

In our setting, we use a natural definition of mass associated to a static triple, following the approach in Chruściel-Herzlich
[
CH
]
, see also
[
Mi
]
.
Let
(M,\widehat{g},V)
be a static triple with
\Lambda=-n
. Furthermore, assume that the Riemannian manifold
(M,\widehat{g})
is asymptotically hyperbolic, in the sense that it is conformally compact and the sectional curvature converges to
-1
at the conformal boundary. See Section
2.1
for a more formal statement.
For a Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
such that the difference
h:=g-\widehat{g}
decays suitably at infinity, we define its
mass
with respect to
(M,\widehat{g},V)
as

\displaystyle m_{\widehat{g},V}{\left(g\right)}:=\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{\partial B_{R}}V\left\langle\mathrm{div}_{\widehat{g}}g-d\tr_{\widehat{g}}g,\widehat{\nu}\right\rangle_{\widehat{g}}+\tr_{\widehat{g}}h\;\widehat{\nu}{\left(V\right)}-h{\left(dV,\widehat{\nu}\right)}\;d\widehat{A},

where
\widehat{\nu}
is the outward-pointing unit normal. This definition of mass can be derived from the linearisation of the scalar curvature as follows: Let
\omega\in\Omega^{1}{\left(M\right)}
be a one-form such that

\displaystyle V\cdot D_{\widehat{g}}\mathrm{scal}(h)=\mathrm{div}_{\widehat{g}}\omega+\left\langle(D_{\widehat{g}}\mathrm{scal})^{*}(V),h\right\rangle_{\widehat{g}},

where
(D_{\widehat{g}}\mathrm{scal})^{*}
is the formal adjoint of
D_{\widehat{g}}\mathrm{scal}
. Then the integrand defining
m_{\widehat{g},V}{\left(g\right)}
is simply
\omega(\nu)
. Note furthermore that the static potential satisfies
(D_{\widehat{g}}\mathrm{scal})^{*}(V)=0
so that Taylor expanding yields

\displaystyle V(\mathrm{scal}_{g}-\mathrm{scal}_{\widehat{g}})=V\cdot D_{\widehat{g}}\mathrm{scal}(h)+V\cdot\mathcal{O}_{2}(\left|h\right|^{2})=\mathrm{div}_{\widehat{g}}\omega+V\cdot\mathcal{O}_{2}(\left|h\right|^{2}),

where
u=\mathcal{O}_{2}{\left(\left|h\right|^{2}\right)}
means
u
and its first two derivatives have such decay. This shows that
m_{\widehat{g},V}{\left(g\right)}
is finite if both
V(\mathrm{scal}_{g}-\mathrm{scal}_{\widehat{g}})
and the remainder term
V\cdot\mathcal{O}_{2}(\left|h\right|^{2})
are integrable. In the asymptotically hyperbolic setting, the static potential
V
usually behaves like
e^{r}
at infinity, so that the mass is finite if
\left|g-\widehat{g}\right|=\mathcal{O}_{2}{\left(e^{-\frac{n}{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}
for some
\varepsilon>0
and
(\mathrm{scal}_{g}-\mathrm{scal}_{\widehat{g}})e^{r}\in L^{1}{\left(M\right)}
.

Remark 1.5
.

It is straightforward to generalise this definition to metrics
g
on different manifolds, provided they are asymptotic to
\widehat{g}
through a diffeomorphism at infinity. Here, we are always working with metrics on a fixed manifold, so we omit this case.

Remark 1.6
.

Later in this paper, we consider the mass as an expression in
\varphi=-\ln{V}
and
h=g-\widehat{g}
for practical purposes. Furthermore, we prefer to work with the sign convention of Besse’s book
[
Be
]
for the divergence and set
\delta_{\widehat{g}}=-\mathrm{div}_{\widehat{g}}
. By slight abuse of notation, we will denote the mass with these substitutions by
m_{\widehat{g}}{\left(h,\varphi\right)}
from Section
3
onwards.

Recall that, on hyperbolic space
{\left(\mathbb{H}^{n},\widehat{g}=g_{\mathbb{H}^{n}}\right)}
, the kernel of
(D_{\widehat{g}}\mathrm{scal})^{*}
is
(n+1)
-dimensional, so that we get an
(n+1)
-dimensional space of static potentials. The kernel can be obtained by embedding
\mathbb{H}^{n}
isometrically into Minkowski space
\mathbb{R}^{1,n}
and restricting the position vector field
\vec{x}=(x^{0},x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
to
\mathbb{H}^{n}
. The restrictions of the component functions
x^{i}
span the kernel of
(D_{\widehat{g}}\mathrm{scal})^{*}
. For this reason, the mass with respect to the hyperbolic metric is defined as an
{\left(n+1\right)}
-dimensional vector
\vec{m}_{\widehat{g},\vec{x}}{\left(g\right)}
, whose components are given by

\displaystyle m_{\widehat{g},x^{i}}{\left(g\right)},\qquad 0\leq i\leq n.

Interpreted as a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{1,n}
, the positive mass theorem by Chrusciel-Herzlich in
[
CH
]
asserts that if the metric
(M,g)
is a complete spin manifold, is asymptotic to
\widehat{g}=g_{\mathbb{H}^{n}}
, and satisfies the inequality
\mathrm{scal}_{g}\geq-n(n-1)
, then the vector
\vec{m}_{\widehat{g},\vec{x}}{\left(g\right)}
is future directed timelike or zero, and the latter case occurs if and only if
g
is isometric to
\widehat{g}
.
To align this statement with our framework, we note that it is equivalent to the following assertion: for each positive function
V\in\ker((D_{\widehat{g}}\mathrm{scal})^{*})
, we have
{m}_{\widehat{g},V}{\left(g\right)}\geq 0
and equality holds if and only if
g
is isometric to
\widehat{g}
.

For a general static triple
(M,\widehat{g},V)
, one cannot expect such a positive mass theorem. On a fixed manifold
M
, one might, however, have a local positive mass property in the following sense:

Definition 1.7
.

Let
(\widehat{g},V)
be an asymptotically hyperbolic static pair. We say that it admits the
local positive mass property
if for all nearby metrics
g
with
\mathrm{scal}_{g}\geq-n{\left(n-1\right)}
, we have

\displaystyle{m}_{\widehat{g},V}{\left(g\right)}\geq 0.

We are going to show that this property is equivalent to dynamical stability of the pair
(\widehat{g},-\ln{V})
as a self-similar solution of the Ricci-harmonic flow. This link is generated by a new entropy functional
\mu_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}(g,\varphi)
for the pair
(g,\varphi)
, which we define in Section
3.1
. Up to diffeomorphism, the Ricci-harmonic flow can be interpreted as a gradient flow of this entropy (in a weighted
L^{2}
-sense).
In the asymptotically hyperbolic setting, subtracting the boundary integral defining
{m}_{\widehat{g},V}
is the right normalisation at infinity to make the entropy well-defined. Such a normalisation has been used in other geometric situations (see
[
DKM
,
DO
]
), in order to define suitable entropies. One of the delicate tasks to deal with here is the fact that the function
\varphi
will not be bounded, but converge to
-\ln{V}
at a suitable rate.
An important cornerstone for the main statements of this paper is the following result which we state roughly here and more precisely in Section
5
:

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
\left(M,\widehat{g},V\right)
be an asymptotically hyperbolic static triple of dimension
n\geq 3
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

The pair
(\widehat{g},-\ln{V})
is a local maximiser of
\mu_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}
,

(ii)

The pair
(\widehat{g},V)
satisfies the local positive mass property.

We also mention that while static metrics each give rise to an
\left(n+1\right)
-dimensional Poincaré–Einstein metric, thereby reducing some of our proofs to those in previous work by the second and third authors (
[
KY
]
), many estimates turn out to require far more work. This is due to the need to ensure the warped product structure of the
\left(n+1\right)
-dimensional Poincaré–Einstein metric is maintained alongside certain curvature conditions. This is a delicate matter and ended up requiring a surprising amount of work compared with the general Poincaré–Einstein case.

Rough statements of the main two results of this article are as follows.

Theorem 1.9
.

(Dynamical stability)
Let
\left(M^{n},\widehat{g},V\right)
be an asymptotically hyperbolic static triple of dimension
n\geq 3
. If
(\widehat{g},-\ln{V})
is a local maximiser of
\mu_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}
, then
for every
L^{2}\cap L^{\infty}
-neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}
of
(\widehat{g},-\ln{V})
, there exists an
H^{\ell}
-neighbourhood
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{U}
(with
\ell>\frac{n}{2}+2
)
such that the Ricci flow starting at any metric in
\mathcal{V}
exists for all times and converges polynomially
(modulo diffeomorphisms) to a static pair in
\mathcal{U}
.

Theorem 1.10
.

(Dynamical instability)
Let
\left(M^{n},\widehat{g},V\right)
be an asymptotically hyperbolic static triple of dimension
n\geq 3
. If
(\widehat{g},-\ln{V})=:\left(\widehat{g},\widehat{\varphi}\right)
is not a local maximiser of
\mu_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}
, then there exists a non–trivial ancient Ricci–harmonic flow
\left\{(g(t),\varphi(t))\right\}_{t\in\left(-\infty,0\right]}
that converges (modulo diffeomorphisms) to
(\widehat{g},\widehat{\varphi})
as
t\to-\infty
.

We refer the reader to Section
6
for the proofs, as well as more detailed and formal statements of the results. Observe that the converse implications also hold due to the monotonicity of
\mu_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}
along the flows. In particular, each static metric is either dynamically stable or dynamically unstable, and this entirely depends on the local behaviour of
\mu_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}
.

Summarising and combining these results and the above discussion, we establish the following equivalences for static solutions:

dynamical stability

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\text{positive mass property for nearby metrics},

dynamical instability

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\text{failure of the positive mass property for nearby metrics}.

Example 1.11
.

Hyperbolic space is well-known to be strictly linearly stable in the sense that the second variation of the entropy is strictly negative in directions orthogonal to diffeomorphisms.
Independently, the positive mass property follows from the positive mass theorem in
[
CH
]
.
There is also an infinite-dimensional family of static triples discovered by Anderson-Chruściel-Delay
[
ACD
]
. These examples arise as static fillings for conformal boundaries close to the round sphere. By continuity, the second variation will still be negative for sufficiently small perturbations, which implies the positive mass property for nearby metrics and dynamical stability due to the results of this article.

An essential ingredient of the dynamical stability and instability theorems is the following Łojasiewicz-Simon inequality for
\mu_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}
:

Theorem 1.12
.

Given a static triple
\left(M^{n},\widehat{g},V\right)
with
n\geq 3
, for
\ell>\frac{n}{2}+2
there is an
H^{\ell}
-neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}
of
{\left(\widehat{g},\widehat{\varphi}\right)}:={\left(\widehat{g},-\ln{V}\right)}
, a positive constant
C>0
, and an exponent
\theta\in\left(0,1\right]
such that for all
{\left(g,\varphi\right)}\in\mathcal{U}
, we have the inequality

\left|\mu_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi\right)}-\mu_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(\widehat{g},\widehat{\varphi}\right)}\right|^{2-\theta}\leq C\left|\left|\nabla\mu_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi\right)}\right|\right|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\widehat{\varphi}}}.

The way Theorem
1.9
and Theorem
1.10
are deduced from Theorem
1.12
follows the approach of the second and third authors’ earlier work in
[
KY
]
for Poincaré-Einstein metrics. In general, the stability proofs in the asymptotically hyperbolic situation are remarkably similar to previous proofs for compact manifolds without boundary.

The paper is structured as follows. Section
2
contains basic definitions and results about asymptotically hyperbolic metrics and static metrics. We also introduce the weighted spaces we will use throughout the paper and present an argument which will show that, under certain decay assumptions on a non-static metric, we can associate to it a generalised ’potential’. In Section
3
, we introduce our expander entropy and show it is well-defined and finite. In Section
4
we compute the first and second variations of the entropy, as well as some related results which include a derivation of the associated stability operator and monotonicity of the entropy under the Ricci-harmonic flow. Section
5
is devoted to the proof of our local positive mass result (Theorem
1.8
). Section
6
contains the proofs of the Łojasiewicz–Simon inequality (Theorem
1.12
), as well as the stability (Theorem
1.9
) and instability (Theorem
1.10
) results. Appendix
A
contains some variational formulas and results we rely on throughout the paper.

Acknowledgements.
The authors would like to thank the Göran Gustafsson Foundation for financial support.

2
Preliminaries

This section contains some basic definitions, results, and identities we will rely on throughout the paper. In particular, we formally introduce asymptotically hyperbolic metrics and static metrics, as well as relationships between
n
-dimensional static metrics and the associated
\left(n+1\right)
-dimensional negative Einstein metrics.

2.1
Asymptotically Hyperbolic Metrics

Definition 2.1
.

Let
N^{n}
be a compact manifold with boundary and set
M=N\setminus\partial N
. A function
\rho:N\to[0,\infty)
is called
boundary defining
, if

\rho|_{M}>0,\qquad\rho|_{\partial N}=0,\qquad d\rho|_{\partial N}\neq 0

Definition 2.2
.

(M,g)
is called
conformally compact of class
C^{\ell,\alpha}
if there exists a
C^{\ell,\alpha}
-metric
\overline{g}
on
N
and a boundary defining function
\rho
, such that
g=\rho^{-2}\overline{g}
. If
\left|d\rho\right|^{2}_{\overline{g}}\cong 1
on
\partial N
,
g
is called
asymptotically hyperbolic (AH)
. We call an Einstein AH metric
Poincaré–Einstein (PE)
. Note this is necessarily a negative Einstein metric as the sectional curvatures converge to
-1
at spatial infinity.

From now on we will implicitly assume the AH manifolds we consider are of class
C^{\ell,\alpha}
for
\ell>\frac{n}{2}+2
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
g
be an AH metric with boundary defining function
\rho
and compactified metric
\overline{g}:=\rho^{2}g
. Then

\rho\Delta_{\overline{g}}\rho=-\frac{\mathrm{scal}_{g}+n(n-1)\left|d\rho\right|_{\overline{g}}^{2}}{2(n-1)}+\frac{\rho^{2}\mathrm{scal}_{\overline{g}}}{2(n-1)}

(2.4)

Proof.

Using a standard formula for the Ricci curvature of conformally related metrics (see, for instance,
[
GL
, Lemma 2.1]
), we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}_{g}={}

\displaystyle-(n-1)\rho^{-2}\left|d\rho\right|^{2}_{\overline{g}}\cdot\overline{g}-\rho^{-1}{\left(\Delta_{\overline{g}}\rho\cdot\overline{g}-(n-2)\nabla^{2}_{\overline{g}}\rho\right)}+\mathrm{Ric}_{\overline{g}}.

Taking the trace with respect to
g=\rho^{-2}\overline{g}
yields (
2.4
).
∎

Remark 2.5
.

Note that, if
\rho
is a boundary defining function on an AH manifold
\left(M,g\right)
, then
r=-\ln{\left(\rho\right)}
is equivalent to a distance function on
\left(M,g\right)
. The volume form of an AH metric satisfies
dV_{g}\sim e^{(n-1)r}\;dr\;dA_{\overline{g}_{r}}
, where
dA_{\overline{g}_{r}}
is the area form of the compactified metric on
\partial B_{r}
. As a consequence, a function
f\sim e^{-\beta r}
is
L^{2}
-integrable if and only if
\beta>\frac{n-1}{2}
.

Remark 2.6
.

Let
g
be an AH metric with boundary defining function
\rho
. Let
\varphi:M\to\mathbb{R}
be a function satisfying

\left|\varphi-\ln\rho\right|\to 0\qquad\&\qquad\left|d\varphi\right|_{g}\to 1

as one approaches
\partial N=\rho^{-1}(0)
. Then
\rho_{\varphi}:=e^{\varphi}
is another boundary defining function, with the same conformal infinity:

{\left(\rho_{\varphi}^{2}g|_{\partial N},\partial N\right)}={\left(\overline{g}|_{\partial N},\partial N\right)}.

Definition 2.7
.

For a pair
(g,\varphi)
as above, we define the associated
auxiliary metric
by

\mathfrak{g}:=e^{-2\varphi}\;d\tau^{2}+g\qquad\text{on}\qquad\mathfrak{M}=\mathbb{S}^{1}\times M,

where
\varphi
is extended trivially along the
\mathbb{S}^{1}
factor. The auxiliary metric is itself AH, with boundary defining function
\rho_{\varphi}
and conformal infinity

{\left(\rho_{\varphi}^{2}\mathfrak{g}|_{\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\partial N},\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\partial N\right)}={\left(d\tau^{2}+\overline{g}|_{\partial N},\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\partial N\right)}.

We will call a function/
1
-form/symmetric
2
-tensor
\tau
-independent
if its restriction to the
\mathbb{S}^{1}
factor is constant.

The importance of the final part of Definition
2.7
is that throughout the paper we will switch between viewing functions, forms, and tensors as either living on M or as
\tau
-independent objects living on
\mathfrak{M}
, depending on what is most convenient.

2.2
Static Metrics and Weighted Hölder and Sobolev Spaces

We now formally define the main objects we are interested in: static metrics.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
{\left(M^{n},\widehat{g}\right)}
be an asymptotically hyperbolic Riemannian metric. We call the metric
\widehat{g}

static
, if there exists a non-trivial solution
V
of

{\left(D_{\widehat{g}}\mathrm{scal}\right)}^{*}{\left(V\right)}={\left(\Delta_{\widehat{g}}V\right)}\widehat{g}+\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{g}}V-V\mathrm{Ric}_{\widehat{g}}=0.

(2.9)

A static metric has constant scalar curvature (see Remark
2.12
below). In particular,
\mathrm{scal}_{\widehat{g}}=-n(n-1)
since
\widehat{g}
is asymptotically hyperbolic. Therefore, (
2.9
) is equivalent to the system

\begin{cases}\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{g}}V=V{\left(\mathrm{Ric}_{\widehat{g}}+n\widehat{g}\right)}\\
\Delta_{\widehat{g}}V=-nV.\end{cases}

We call
V
a
static potential
for
\widehat{g}
,
\left(\widehat{g},V\right)
a
static pair
, and
\left(M,\widehat{g},V\right)
a
static triple
.

If the potential
V
is positive near the conformal boundary, it is positive everywhere by a maximum principle argument. It then makes sense to consider the equations of staticity under the transformation
\widehat{\varphi}=-\ln{V}
:

\begin{cases}\nabla^{2}_{\widehat{g}}\widehat{\varphi}-d\widehat{\varphi}\otimes d\widehat{\varphi}+\mathrm{Ric}_{\widehat{g}}+n\widehat{g}=0\\
\Delta_{\widehat{g}}\widehat{\varphi}+\left|d\widehat{\varphi}\right|_{\widehat{g}}^{2}=n.\end{cases}

(2.10)

The reader should be aware, that when we refer to
\widehat{\varphi}
as a static potential,
{\left(\widehat{g},\widehat{\varphi}\right)}
a static pair, or
{\left(M,\widehat{g},\widehat{\varphi}\right)}
as static triple, it should always be interpreted as the potential
V=e^{-\widehat{\varphi}}
in the above sense.

Remark 2.11
.

If
(\widehat{g},V)
is a static pair, both the
(n+1)
-dimensional Lorentzian metric

h=-V^{2}\;d\tau^{2}+\widehat{g}

as well as the
(n+1)
-dimensional Riemannian metric

h=V^{2}\;d\tau^{2}+\widehat{g}

solve the equation
\mathrm{Ric}_{h}=-nh
. In particular, the latter metric is Poincaré-Einstein.

Remark 2.12
.

Taking the divergence of (
2.9
), we obtain

0=-\nabla_{\widehat{g}}\Delta_{\widehat{g}}V+\Delta_{\widehat{g}}\nabla_{\widehat{g}}V+\mathrm{Ric}_{\widehat{g}}{\left(\nabla_{\widehat{g}}V,\cdot\right)}-V\delta_{\widehat{g}}\mathrm{Ric}_{\widehat{g}}=\frac{V}{2}\nabla_{\widehat{g}}\mathrm{scal}_{\widehat{g}},

where the second equality is a result of using the Ricci formula and the contracted Bianchi identity. It follows that a static metric must have constant scalar curvature, at least on each connected component of
M
.

Example 2.13
.

Consider the standard hyperbolic metric on
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, written as

g_{\mathbb{H}^{n}}=dr^{2}+\sinh^{2}{r}\;ds^{2}_{n-1},

with
ds_{n-1}^{2}
the round metric on
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
. In these coordinates, a static potential for
g_{\mathbb{H}^{n}}
is

V_{\mathbb{H}^{n}}:=2\cosh{r}.

Note that this means

\varphi_{\mathbb{H}^{n}}=-\ln{\left(V_{\mathbb{H}^{n}}\right)}=-\ln{\left(e^{r}+e^{-r}\right)}=-r+\ln{\left(1+e^{-2r}\right)}=-r+\mathcal{O}(e^{-2r})

as
r\rightarrow\infty
.

The corresponding static solution to Einstein’s equations is the
(n+1)
-dimensional Anti-de Sitter metric. In Riemannian form, using the same coordinates as above, we have

g_{AdS}=V_{\mathbb{H}^{n}}^{2}\;d\tau^{2}+g_{\mathbb{H}^{n}}=dr^{2}+\cosh^{2}{r}\;d\tau^{2}+\sinh^{2}{r}\;ds_{n-1}^{2}.

Motivated by the above example, from now on we will impose that
the potentials
\varphi
we will consider are asymptotic to
-r
and of regularity
C^{\ell,\alpha}
(at least)
, regardless of whether they are associated to a static metric or not.

Moreover, we will often work with weighted Hölder and Sobolev spaces. In particular, proofs in future sections will involve working with
C^{\ell,\alpha}_{\beta}{\left(M\right)}=e^{-\beta r}C^{\ell,\alpha}{\left(M\right)}
equipped with the norm

\left|\left|u\right|\right|_{C^{\ell,\alpha}_{\beta}{\left(M\right)}}=\left|\left|e^{\beta r}u\right|\right|_{C^{\ell,\alpha}{\left(M\right)}},

where
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
and
C^{\ell,\alpha}{\left(M\right)}
is the usual Hölder space. As for weighted Sobolev spaces, the only weight we shall consider is
\frac{1}{2}
. However, as
\varphi
is always asymptotic to
-r
, we shall use the weight factor
e^{\frac{\varphi}{2}}
in place of
e^{-\frac{r}{2}}
. Thus we define
H^{\ell}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}
to be the standard
L^{2}
-Sobolev space, defined with respect to the weighted volume measure
e^{-\varphi}dV_{g}
. Weighted Hölder and Sobolev spaces for sections of bundles are then defined as in, for instance,
[
Lee
]
. We also have the following version of the Sobolev embedding theorem for our weighted spaces (see
[
Lee
, Lemma 3.6]
):

Lemma 2.14
.

If
\alpha\in\left(0,1\right)
and
E
is a vector bundle over
M
, then we have a continuous inclusion

H^{\ell}_{\varphi}{\left(M;E\right)}\hookrightarrow C^{2,\alpha}_{\frac{1}{2}}{\left(M;E\right)}

provided
\ell>\frac{n}{2}+2
.

We shall usually omit the manifold
M
, as exemplified immediately below, as long as we deem it clear from context.

We will denote the space of metrics that are
C^{\ell,\alpha}_{\beta}
-close to a static background metric
\widehat{g}
by

\mathcal{R}^{\ell,\alpha}_{\beta}{\left(M,\widehat{g}\right)}:=\left\{g:g-\widehat{g}\in C^{\ell,\alpha}_{\beta}{\left(S^{2}M\right)}\right\},

and if
\widehat{\varphi}
is a static potential for
\widehat{g}
, we shall denote the space of metrics that are
H^{\ell}_{\widehat{\varphi}}
-close to
\widehat{g}
by

\mathcal{R}^{\ell}_{\widehat{\varphi}}{\left(M,\widehat{g}\right)}:=\left\{g:g-\widehat{g}\in H^{\ell}_{\widehat{\varphi}}{\left(S^{2}M\right)}\right\}.

Moreover, we will sometimes write the space of functions that are
H^{\ell}_{\widehat{\varphi}}{\left(M\right)}
-close to
\widehat{\varphi}
as

\mathcal{R}^{\ell}_{\widehat{\varphi}}{\left(M,\widehat{\varphi}\right)}:=\left\{\varphi:\varphi-\widehat{\varphi}\in H^{\ell}_{\widehat{\varphi}}{\left(M\right)}\right\}.

2.3
Static Metrics and the Corresponding
(n+1)
-Dimensional Negative Einstein Metrics

Throughout this article, we will consider pairs
{\left(g,\varphi\right)}
, where
g
is an AH Riemannian metric on an
n
-dimensional manifold
M
, and
\varphi
is an
approximate potential
for
g
in the sense of Lemma
2.22
below. The lemma gives more precise asymptotic behaviour than simply being asymptotic to
-r
.

Dual to this picture of a pair
{\left(g,\varphi\right)}
is the
(n+1)
-dimensional
auxiliary manifold

{\left(\mathfrak{M}=\mathbb{S}^{1}\times M,\mathfrak{g}\right)}\qquad\text{with}\qquad\mathfrak{g}=e^{-2\varphi}\;d\tau^{2}+g,

where
\tau\in\mathbb{S}^{1}=\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
. The added dimension has to be compactified, so as to make
\mathfrak{g}
asymptotically hyperbolic, but nowhere is it essential that the length of the circle is chosen to be
2\pi
.

The non-vanishing Christoffel symbols of
\mathfrak{g}
satisfy

\overline{\Gamma}_{\tau\tau}^{i}=e^{-2\varphi}g^{ij}d\varphi_{j},\qquad\overline{\Gamma}_{\tau i}^{\tau}=-d\varphi_{i},\qquad\overline{\Gamma}_{ij}^{k}=\Gamma_{ij}^{k}\qquad\qquad{\left(i,j,k\neq\tau\right)},

where
\overline{\Gamma}_{ij}^{k}
and
\Gamma_{ij}^{k}
denote the Christoffel symbols of
\mathfrak{g}
and
g
, respectively. The Ricci curvature may now be calculated. For the
\tau\tau
component, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tau\tau}={}

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\overline{\Gamma}_{\tau\tau}^{i}+\Gamma_{im}^{i}\overline{\Gamma}_{\tau\tau}^{m}-\overline{\Gamma}_{i\tau}^{\tau}\overline{\Gamma}_{\tau\tau}^{i}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}{\left(e^{-2\varphi}g^{ij}d\varphi_{j}\right)}+e^{-2\varphi}g^{ij}d\varphi_{i}d\varphi_{j}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle e^{-2\varphi}{\left(g^{ij}\nabla^{2}_{ij}\varphi-g^{ij}d\varphi_{i}d\varphi_{j}\right)}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-e^{-2\varphi}{\left(\Delta_{g}\varphi+\left|d\varphi\right|_{g}^{2}\right)}.

For
i,j\neq\tau
we find that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{ij}={}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}_{ij}^{g}-\partial_{i}\overline{\Gamma}_{j\tau}^{\tau}+\Gamma_{ij}^{k}\overline{\Gamma}_{k\tau}^{\tau}-\overline{\Gamma}_{\tau i}^{\tau}\overline{\Gamma}_{j\tau}^{\tau}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}_{ij}^{g}-\nabla_{i}\overline{\Gamma}_{j\tau}^{\tau}-\overline{\Gamma}_{\tau i}^{\tau}\overline{\Gamma}_{j\tau}^{\tau}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}_{ij}^{g}+\nabla^{2}_{ij}\varphi-d\varphi_{i}d\varphi_{j}.

Written invariantly, we have

\mathrm{Ric}_{\mathfrak{g}}=-{\left(\Delta_{g}\varphi+\left|d\varphi\right|_{g}^{2}\right)}e^{-2\varphi}\;d\tau^{2}+\mathrm{Ric}_{g}+\nabla^{2}_{g}\varphi-d\varphi\otimes d\varphi.

(2.15)

Taking the trace with respect to
\mathfrak{g}
, we obtain

\mathrm{scal}_{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathrm{scal}_{g}-2\Delta_{g}\varphi-2\left|d\varphi\right|_{g}^{2}.

One thus sees, as in Remark
2.11
and Remark
2.12
, that if
{\left(g,\varphi\right)}
is a static pair then
\mathrm{Ric}_{\mathfrak{g}}=-n\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathrm{scal}_{\mathfrak{g}}=-n{\left(n+1\right)}
.

Identities

We now record some important identities relating some standard differential operators on
M
, with the corresponding operators on
\mathfrak{M}
, acting on
\tau
-independent objects.

For
\left|s\right|\ll 1
, let
g_{s}
,
\varphi_{s}
be families of, respectively, AH metrics and approximate potentials with associated auxiliary metrics
\mathfrak{g}_{s}
. Next, we define
h:=\left.\frac{d}{ds}\right|_{s=0}g_{s}
,
\psi:=\left.\frac{d}{ds}\right|_{s=0}\varphi_{s}
, and
\mathfrak{h}:=\left.\frac{d}{ds}\right|_{s=0}\mathfrak{g}_{s}
.

We shall simultaneously view
\mathfrak{h}
as a section of
S^{2}M\times C^{\infty}(M)
and a section of
S^{2}\mathfrak{M}
. Consequently, we write

\mathfrak{h}={\left(h,\psi\right)}=-2\psi e^{-2\varphi}\;d\tau^{2}+h.

(2.16)

Observe, in particular, the factor
-2
on the first term, as it plays a role in how we interpret the norm and vector product of such pairs.

1.

Consider
\omega\in\Omega^{1}{\left(M\right)}
and view it as a
\tau
-independent
1
-form on
\mathfrak{M}
. The divergences with respect to
g
and
\mathfrak{g}
are related by

\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}\omega=\delta_{g}\omega+\left\langle d\varphi,\omega\right\rangle.

The right hand side will be denoted by
\delta_{\varphi}
when we want to emphasise the role of
\varphi
. We remark that
{\left(\delta_{\varphi}\right)}^{*}=d
, with respect to the auxiliary/weighted volume
dV_{\mathfrak{g}}=e^{-\varphi}\;dV
.

2.

Let
f:M\to\mathbb{R}
and consider it as a
\tau
-independent function on
\mathfrak{M}
. The Laplacian of
\mathfrak{g}
applied to
f
is the drift Laplacian

\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}f=\Delta_{g}f+\left\langle d\varphi,df\right\rangle,

(2.17)

which shall also be denoted by
\Delta_{\varphi}
when the metric
g
is clear from the context.

3.

Let
\mathfrak{h}
be as above. Then

\displaystyle\tr_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathfrak{h}

\displaystyle=\tr_{g}h-2\psi,

(2.18)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathfrak{h}

\displaystyle=\delta_{g}h+h\cdot d\varphi+2\psi\;d\varphi.

(2.19)

Here, and moving forward,
h\cdot d\varphi:=h{\left(d\varphi,\cdot\right)}
. When
h
is merely a tensor on
M
, e.g. a variation of the base metric
g
, we shall use the notation
\delta_{\varphi}h=\delta_{g}h+h\cdot d\varphi
for the natural shift divergence.

4.

Let
X
be a vector field on
M
and consider it as a
\tau
-independent vector field on
\mathfrak{M}
. We then have the following relation between codivergences:

\delta^{*}_{\mathfrak{g}}X=-X(\varphi)e^{-2\varphi}\;d\tau^{2}+\delta^{*}_{g}X.

Specifically, if
\mathfrak{h}=\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}X=\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{L}_{X}\mathfrak{g}
, then it is dual to the pair
{\left(h,\psi\right)}={\left(\delta^{*}_{g}X,\frac{1}{2}X(\varphi)\right)}={\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{L}_{X}g,\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{L}_{X}\varphi\right)}
. The factor
\frac{1}{2}
is the result of the
-2
in the interpretation of the pair, given by (
2.16
).

Relating Norms on
M
and
\mathfrak{M}

Later on, in the proofs of our stability and instability results, it will be useful to know Hölder and Sobolev norms on
M
and
\mathfrak{M}
are equivalent when dealing with
\tau
-independent objects.

Let
f
denote a real valued function on
M
, simultaneously viewed as a
\tau
-independent function
f:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathfrak{M}=\mathbb{S}^{1}\times M
. Let
\mathfrak{g}
be the auxiliary metric associated to
(g,\varphi)
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\left|\left|f\right|\right|_{L^{2}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathfrak{M})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left|\left|f\right|\right|_{L^{2}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}}^{2}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\left|\left|f\right|\right|_{H^{1}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathfrak{M})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left|\left|f\right|\right|_{H^{1}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}}^{2}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\left|\left|f\right|\right|_{H^{2}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathfrak{M})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left|\left|f\right|\right|_{H^{2}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}}^{2}+{\left(df,d\varphi\right)}^{2}_{L^{2}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}}.

Let
\mathfrak{h}=(h,\psi)
denote an auxiliary
2
-tensor, as in (
2.16
), then the pointwise norms satisfy

\displaystyle\left|\mathfrak{h}\right|^{2}_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle=\left|(h,\psi)\right|^{2}_{g},

\displaystyle\left|\nabla_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathfrak{h}\right|^{2}_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle=\left|\nabla_{g}\mathfrak{h}\right|_{g}^{2}+2\left|\mathfrak{h}\cdot d\varphi\right|_{g}^{2},

where
\nabla_{g}\mathfrak{h}={\left(\nabla_{g}h,\nabla_{g}\psi\right)}
,

\mathfrak{h}\cdot d\varphi=h\cdot d\varphi+2\psi\;d\varphi,

and
g
should be understood as acting on a pair of tensors
(T_{1},T_{2})
by

\left|{\left(T_{1},T_{2}\right)}\right|^{2}_{g}=\left|T_{1}\right|_{g}^{2}+4\left|T_{2}\right|^{2}_{g}.

More generally, we have

\left|\nabla^{\ell}_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathfrak{h}\right|^{2}_{\mathfrak{g}}=\left|\nabla^{\ell}_{g}\mathfrak{h}\right|_{g}^{2}+\sum_{i+j=0}^{\ell-1}\left|\nabla^{i}_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathfrak{h}\ast\nabla^{j}_{g}d\varphi\right|_{g}^{2},

where
\ast
is allowed to express any linear combination of contractions of the tensors involved. As such contractions only influence normed inequalities by a multiplicative factor, we shall rely heavily on this notation in the latter parts of the article.
From the pointwise equality, it follows that

\left|\left|\mathfrak{h}\right|\right|_{H^{\ell}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\left|\left|\mathfrak{h}\right|\right|_{H^{\ell}_{\mathfrak{g}}{\left(\mathfrak{M}\right)}}^{2}\leq\left|\left|\mathfrak{h}\right|\right|_{H^{\ell}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}}^{2}+C_{\ell}\left|\left|\mathfrak{h}\right|\right|^{2}_{H^{\ell-1}_{\mathfrak{g}}{\left(\mathfrak{M}\right)}}\leq D_{\ell}\left|\left|\mathfrak{h}\right|\right|_{H^{\ell}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}}^{2},

(2.20)

where the third inequality follows from repeated application of the second, and
C_{\ell},D_{\ell}
depend on the
C^{m}{\left(M\right)}
-norm of
d\varphi
for
0\leq m\leq\ell-1
(Recall that we implicitly assume that
\varphi\in C^{\ell,\alpha}(M)
). An analogous calculation gives

\left|\left|\mathfrak{h}\right|\right|_{C^{\ell}_{g}{\left(M\right)}}^{2}\leq\left|\left|\mathfrak{h}\right|\right|_{C^{\ell}_{\mathfrak{g}}{\left(\mathfrak{M}\right)}}^{2}\leq E_{\ell}\left|\left|\mathfrak{h}\right|\right|_{C^{\ell}_{g}{\left(M\right)}}^{2},

where
E_{\ell}
has the same dependencies as
C_{\ell}
and
D_{\ell}
. To simplify notation moving forward, we will omit the manifolds
M
and
\mathfrak{M}
when writing norms, as long as it is clear from context which manifold we are working with.

Decomposition of Symmetric
2
-Tensors

Let
(M,g,\varphi)
be an AH triple, such that the auxiliary manifold
{\left(\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{g}\right)}
is AH as well. Then, for
0<\beta<n
, we have the common splitting

C_{\beta}^{\ell,\alpha}{\left(S^{2}\mathfrak{M}\right)}=\ker\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}|_{C_{\beta}^{\ell,\alpha}{\left(S^{2}\mathfrak{M}\right)}}\oplus_{L^{2}}\mathrm{Im}\;\delta^{*}_{\mathfrak{g}}|_{C^{\ell+1,\alpha}_{\beta}(T\mathfrak{M})}.

(2.21)

The range of allowable weights,
\beta
, is determined by the Fredholm range of
\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}
, which is exactly
(0,n)
.

Additionally, if
\mathfrak{g}
is a negative Poincaré–Einstein metric (that is, if
(g,\varphi)
is static), the linearised scalar curvature induces the splitting

C_{\beta}^{\ell,\alpha}{\left(S^{2}\mathfrak{M}\right)}=\ker D_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathrm{scal}|_{C_{\beta}^{\ell,\alpha}(S^{2}\mathfrak{M})}\oplus_{L^{2}}\mathrm{Im}\;{\left(D_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathrm{scal}\right)}^{*}|_{C^{\ell+2,\alpha}_{\beta}(\mathfrak{M})}.

Note that the second factor of the first splitting is contained in the first factor of the second, and vice versa. Thus they combine to yield the orthogonal decomposition

C_{\beta}^{\ell,\alpha}{\left(S^{2}\mathfrak{M}\right)}=TT_{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus_{L^{2}}\mathrm{Im}\;{\left(D_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathrm{scal}\right)}^{*}|_{C^{\ell+2,\alpha}_{\beta}(\mathfrak{M})}\oplus_{L^{2}}\mathrm{Im}\;\delta^{*}_{\mathfrak{g}}|_{C^{\ell+1,\alpha}_{\beta}(T\mathfrak{M})},

where

TT_{\mathfrak{g}}=\ker D_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathrm{scal}|_{C_{\beta}^{\ell,\alpha}(S^{2}\mathfrak{M})}\cap\ker\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}|_{C_{\beta}^{\ell,\alpha}(S^{2}\mathfrak{M})}

is the space of transverse–trace free tensors.

2.4
Existence of Approximate Potentials

Since we are interested in metric-potential pairs
(g,\varphi)
that are not necessarily static, we shall need to weaken the concept of a static potential. The following lemma provides the definition, existence and asymptotic uniqueness of such potentials, for any AH metric whose scalar curvature decays fast enough.

Lemma 2.22
.

Let
g
be an AH metric with boundary defining function
\rho=e^{-r}
, satisfying
\mathrm{scal}_{g}+n(n-1)=\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-\beta r}\right)}
, for some
\frac{n}{2}<\beta<n
. Then there exists a smooth function
\varphi
, asymptotic to
-r
, such that

\Delta\varphi+\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}=n+\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-\beta r}\right)}.

(2.23)

Moreover, this function is unique modulo
\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-\beta r}\right)}
, and if
\widehat{g}
is another AH metric with the same conformal infinity and
\left|g-\widehat{g}\right|,\left|\mathrm{scal}_{\widehat{g}}+n(n-1)\right|=\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-\beta r}\right)}
, then
\varphi
solves (
2.23
) with respect to
\widehat{g}
.

A function satisfying (
2.23
) and
\varphi\to-r
, as
r\to\infty
, shall be called an
approximate potential
for
g
. We dedicate the notation
\varphi
solely to such functions.

Proof.

Choosing a sufficiently large compact subset
K\subset M
, we can make the identification
M\setminus K\simeq(R,\infty)\times\partial N
, and write
g
in the form

g=dr^{2}+e^{2r}h_{r},

where
h_{r}
is a one-parameter family of metrics on
\partial N
. The Laplacian of
g
can then be expressed as

\Delta\psi=-\partial^{2}_{rr}\psi-{\left(n-1+G\right)}\partial_{r}\psi+e^{-2r}\Delta_{h_{r}}\psi,

where
G=\frac{1}{2}\tr_{h_{r}}\partial_{r}h_{r}
. Suppose
\varphi
is a function solving (
2.23
). This is equivalent to
\psi=\varphi+r
satisfying

\Delta\psi+\left|d\psi\right|^{2}=2\partial_{r}\psi-G(r)+\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-\beta r}\right)}.

(2.24)

Note that
G(r)=G(-\ln{\rho})
equals the left hand side of (
2.4
). So, by the assumption on
\mathrm{scal}_{g}
, we have

G(r)=\frac{1}{2(n-1)}e^{-2r}\mathrm{scal}_{\overline{g}}+\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-\beta r}\right)}.

As
\beta>\frac{n}{2}\geq 1
, and we are only interested in lower order terms, we may choose an asymptotic expansion of
\mathrm{scal}_{\overline{g}}
such that

G(r,x)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\lceil\beta\rceil}b_{\ell}(x)e^{-\ell r}+\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-\beta r}\right)},

(2.25)

where
x
indicates tangential coordinates on
\partial N
.
Suppose
\psi=\psi(r,x)
is a solution of (
2.24
). That is,

-\psi^{\prime\prime}-(n+1)\psi^{\prime}+{\left(\psi^{\prime}\right)}^{2}-G(\psi^{\prime}-1)+e^{-2r}{\left(\Delta_{h_{r}}\psi+\left|d\psi\right|^{2}_{h_{r}}\right)}=\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-\beta r}\right)},

where
\psi^{\prime}=\partial_{r}\psi
. By inserting
\psi=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}a_{\ell}(x)e^{-\ell r}
and using (
2.25
), we obtain

\displaystyle 0=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\lceil\beta\rceil}{\left(\ell(n+1-\ell)a_{\ell}+b_{\ell}\right)}e^{-\ell r}+\sum_{\ell+m=0}^{\lceil\beta\rceil}\ell a_{\ell}(b_{m}+ma_{m})e^{-(\ell+m)r}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\ell=2}^{\lceil\beta\rceil}{\left(\Delta_{h}a_{\ell-2}\right)}e^{-\ell r}+\sum_{\ell+m=2}^{\lceil\beta\rceil}\left\langle da_{\ell-1},da_{m-1}\right\rangle_{h}e^{-(\ell+m)r}.

Recall that
b_{0}=0
by (
2.25
), so the equation above gives the following recurrence relations for the coefficients
a_{\ell}
:

a_{0}=0,\quad a_{1}=-\frac{b_{1}}{n},\quad a_{2}=-\frac{b_{2}}{2(n-1)}+\frac{b_{1}^{2}}{2n^{2}}

and for
\ell\geq 3
:

\ell(n+1-\ell)a_{\ell}+b_{\ell}+\sum_{m+p=\ell}ma_{m}{\left(b_{p}+pa_{p}\right)}+\Delta_{h}a_{\ell-2}+\sum_{m+p=\ell-2}\left\langle da_{m},da_{p}\right\rangle_{h}=0.

These recurrence relations are uniquely solvable for all
\ell\leq\lceil\beta\rceil<n+1
. Setting
\varphi{\left(r\right)}=-r+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\lceil\beta\rceil}a_{\ell}e^{-\ell r}
, we obtain a solution to (
2.23
).

Suppose
\widehat{g}
is another AH metric with
\left|g-\widehat{g}\right|,\left|\mathrm{scal}_{\widehat{g}}+n(n-1)\right|=\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-\beta r}\right)}
and the same conformal infinity. Then, by using (
2.4
), we see that (
2.24
) agrees with the corresponding equation with respect to
\widehat{g}
, up to relevant order.
∎

Remark 2.26
.

It is instructive to compare the functions in the proof of Lemma
2.22
to those associated with hyperbolic space:

\varphi_{\mathbb{H}^{n}}=-\ln{\left(2\cosh{r}\right)}=-r+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{\ell}e^{-2\ell r},

G_{\mathbb{H}^{n}}=(n-1){\left(\coth{r}-1\right)}=2{\left(n-1\right)}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}e^{-2\ell r}.

In this case the potential is not only approximate, it is static.
From the proof of
2.22
, it is clear that if
G=\mathcal{O}(e^{-\ell r})
then
\varphi+r=\mathcal{O}(e^{-\ell r})
.

2.4.1
Isomorphism Properties of Laplace Type Operators

We will extensively use the following proposition throughout the paper.

Proposition 2.27
.

Let
{\left(M,g\right)}
be an AH manifold of dimension
n
and class
C^{\ell,\alpha}
with approximate potential function
\varphi
. Then

\displaystyle\Delta_{g}+n:H^{m}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}

\displaystyle\rightarrow H^{m-2}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)},

\displaystyle\Delta_{\varphi}+n:H^{m}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}

\displaystyle\rightarrow H^{m-2}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}

are isomorphisms for
m=2,\dots,\ell
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\Delta_{g}+n:C^{m,\alpha}_{\beta}{\left(M\right)}

\displaystyle\rightarrow C^{m-2,\alpha}_{\beta}{\left(M\right)},

\displaystyle\Delta_{\varphi}+n:C^{m,\alpha}_{\gamma}{\left(M\right)}

\displaystyle\rightarrow C^{m-2,\alpha}_{\gamma}{\left(M\right)}

are isomorphisms for
m=2,\dots,\ell
,
\beta\in{\left(-1,n\right)}
, and
\gamma\in{\left(\frac{n}{2}-\sqrt{\frac{n^{2}}{4}+n},\frac{n}{2}+\sqrt{\frac{n^{2}}{4}+n}\right)}
.

Proof.

By
[
Lee
, Proposition F & Corollary 7.4]
, the indicial radius of
\Delta_{g}+n
is
R=\sqrt{\frac{{\left(n-1\right)}^{2}}{4}+n}=\frac{n+1}{2}
. Since
\left|\frac{1}{2}\right|<\frac{n+1}{2}
, we can apply
[
Lee
, Theorem C]
to see that, for
m=2,\dots,\ell
,

\Delta_{g}+n:H^{m}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}\rightarrow H^{m-2}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}

is a Fredholm operator of index
0
and its kernel agrees with the
L^{2}
-kernel of
\Delta_{g}+n
. However, the
L^{2}
-kernel of
\Delta_{g}+n
is trivial, hence
\Delta_{g}+n
is an isomorphism between the Sobolev spaces detailed above.

Similarly, since
\left|\beta-\frac{n-1}{2}\right|<\frac{n+1}{2}
if and only if
\beta\in{\left(-1,n\right)}
, we can use
[
Lee
, Theorem C]
and that
\Delta_{g}+n
has trivial
L^{2}
-kernel to deduce that

\Delta_{g}+n:C^{m,\alpha}_{\beta}{\left(M\right)}\rightarrow C^{m-2,\alpha}_{\beta}{\left(M\right)}

is an isomorphism for
m=2,\dots,\ell
and
\beta
in the given range.

As for the statements involving
\Delta_{\varphi}
, we conjugate by the multiplication operator of
e^{\frac{\varphi}{2}}

e^{-\frac{\varphi}{2}}\circ{\left(\Delta_{\varphi}+n\right)}\circ e^{\frac{\varphi}{2}}=\Delta_{g}+F(\varphi)

where
F{\left(\varphi\right)}=\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{g}\varphi+\frac{1}{4}\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}+n=\frac{6n-1}{4}+\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-2r}\right)}
. This means

\Delta_{\varphi}+n:C^{m,\alpha}_{\gamma}{\left(M\right)}\to C^{m-2,\alpha}_{\gamma}{\left(M\right)}

is an isomorphism if and only if

\Delta_{g}+\frac{6n-1}{4}:C^{m,\alpha}_{\gamma-\frac{1}{2}}{\left(M\right)}\to C^{m-2,\alpha}_{\gamma-\frac{1}{2}}{\left(M\right)}

is an isomorphism. By
[
Lee
, Proposition F, Corollary 7.4 & Theorem C]
, this is the case whenever

\left|\gamma-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{n-1}{2}\right|=\left|\gamma-\frac{n}{2}\right|<\sqrt{\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4}+\frac{6n-1}{4}}=\sqrt{\frac{n^{2}}{4}+n}.

Similarly

\Delta_{\varphi}+n:H^{m}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}\to H^{m-2}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}

is an isomorphism if and only if

\Delta_{g}+\frac{6n-1}{4}:H^{m}{\left(M\right)}\to H^{m-2}{\left(M\right)}

is an isomorphism. The latter is the case, as the indicial radius
R=\sqrt{\frac{n^{2}}{4}+n}
of
\Delta_{g}+\frac{6n-1}{4}
is positive. Note that this is exactly the radius of the auxiliary operator
\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}+n
(see also (
2.17
)).
∎

3
A Renormalised Expander Entropy

This section contains the definition of our entropy functional, as well as the proof that it is well-defined and finite. We also prove that a minimising function of the entropy exists and has asymptotic properties suitable for our future purposes.

3.1
Definition of the Entropy

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\widehat{g}
be a static background metric. Then define the following functional:

S_{\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi\right)}:=\lim\limits_{R\rightarrow\infty}\left[\int_{B_{R}}\mathrm{scal}_{g}+n(n-1)\;d\widehat{V}_{\varphi}-m_{\widehat{g},R}{\left(g-\widehat{g},\varphi\right)}\right],

where
m_{\widehat{g},R}
is the
weighted ADM mass
term

m_{\widehat{g},R}{\left(h,\varphi\right)}:=-\int_{\partial B_{R}}\left\langle\delta_{\widehat{g}}h+d\tr_{\widehat{g}}h,\widehat{\nu}\right\rangle_{\widehat{g}}+\tr_{\widehat{g}}h\;\widehat{\nu}{\left(\varphi\right)}-h{\left(d\varphi,\widehat{\nu}\right)}\;d\widehat{A}_{\varphi},

(3.2)

where
d\widehat{V}_{\varphi}=e^{-\varphi}\;dV_{\widehat{g}}
, and
d\widehat{A}_{\varphi}=e^{-\varphi}\;dA_{\widehat{g}}
. We also define the following boundary term:

\mathcal{B}_{R}{\left(g,\varphi,f\right)}:=-\frac{1}{n}\int_{\partial B_{R}}{\left(f-\varphi\right)}\nu{\left(\varphi\right)}dA_{\varphi}.

We note that by setting
\widehat{g}=g_{\mathbb{H}^{n}}
in (
3.2
), we obtain the mass previously studied in
[
CH
]
and
[
HJM
]
.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
{\left(M,g\right)}
be an AH manifold and let
f,\varphi\in C^{\infty}{\left(M\right)}
be functions that are asymptotic to
-r
. Moreover, let
\widehat{g}
be a static background metric and define the functionals

\mathcal{W}_{R}{\left(g,\varphi,f\right)}:=\int_{B_{R}}\frac{1}{2n}{\left(\left|df\right|^{2}-\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}+\mathrm{scal}_{g}\right)}+\varphi-f+\frac{n-1}{2}\;dV_{f}

and

\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi,f\right)}:=\lim\limits_{R\rightarrow\infty}{\left(\mathcal{W}_{R}{\left(g,\varphi,f\right)}-\frac{1}{2n}m_{\widehat{g},R}{\left(g-\widehat{g},\varphi\right)}+\mathcal{B}_{R}{\left(g,\varphi,f\right)}\right)},

where
dV_{f}:=e^{-f}dV_{g}
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
{\left(M,g\right)}
be an AH manifold with approximate potential
\varphi
(satisfying (
2.23
)). Further let
\widehat{g}
be a static background metric with

\mathrm{scal}_{\widehat{g}}+n(n-1)=0.

If
g\in\mathcal{R}^{2,\alpha}_{\beta}{\left(M,\widehat{g}\right)}
, for some
\frac{n}{2}<\beta<n
, then
S_{\widehat{g}}(g,\varphi)
is finite. As a result, under these assumptions,
m_{\widehat{g}}{\left(g-\widehat{g},\varphi\right)}:=\lim\limits_{R\rightarrow\infty}m_{\widehat{g},R}{\left(g-\widehat{g},\varphi\right)}
is well-defined and finite.

Proof.

By Taylor expanding
\mathrm{scal}_{g}
around
\widehat{g}
in the direction of
h=g-\widehat{g}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}\mathrm{scal}_{g}+n(n-1)\;d\widehat{V}_{\varphi}={}

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}\mathrm{scal}_{\widehat{g}}+n(n-1)+D_{\widehat{g}}\mathrm{scal}_{\widehat{g}}(h)\;d\widehat{V}_{\varphi}+C_{R}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle m_{\widehat{g},R}{\left(h,\varphi\right)}-\int_{B_{R}}\left\langle Q\varphi,h\right\rangle\;d\widehat{V}_{\varphi}+C_{R},

where

Q\varphi=-e^{\varphi}{\left(D_{\widehat{g}}\mathrm{scal}\right)}^{*}{\left(e^{-\varphi}\right)}={\left(\Delta_{\widehat{g}}\varphi+\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}_{\widehat{g}}\right)}\cdot\widehat{g}+\nabla_{\widehat{g}}^{2}\varphi-d\varphi\otimes d\varphi+\mathrm{Ric}_{\widehat{g}},

and
C_{R}
denotes an integral of quadratic terms in
h
and
\nabla h
. As such terms decay faster than the weighted volume grows, we have
\lim_{R\to\infty}\left|C_{R}\right|<\infty
.

Let
\widehat{\varphi}
be a static potential for
\widehat{g}
asymptotic to
-r
. Then it satisfies the defining equation for static potentials:
Q\widehat{\varphi}=0
. Taking the trace, we also have

0=\tr Q\widehat{\varphi}=(n-1){\left(\Delta_{\widehat{g}}\widehat{\varphi}+\left|d\widehat{\varphi}\right|_{\widehat{g}}^{2}-n\right)}.

It then follows from Lemma
2.22
that
\varphi-\widehat{\varphi}=\mathcal{O}(e^{-\beta r})
. As a consequence, we have
\left|Q\varphi\right|=\left|Q(\varphi-\widehat{\varphi})\right|=\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-\beta r}\right)}
. It follows from the decay assumption on
h=g-\widehat{g}
, that

S_{\widehat{g}}(g,\varphi)=\lim_{R\to\infty}{\left(C_{R}-\int_{B_{R}}\left\langle Q\varphi,h\right\rangle\;d\widehat{V}_{\varphi}\right)}

is finite.
∎

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
g\in\mathcal{R}^{2,\alpha}_{\beta}{\left(M,\widehat{g}\right)}
have approximate potential
\varphi
. If
f-\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}{\left(M\right)}
, then
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi,f\right)}
is finite.

Proof.

From the definitions of
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}
and
S_{\widehat{g}}
, one can compute that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}(g,\varphi,f)={}&\frac{1}{2n}\lim\limits_{R\rightarrow\infty}\bigg[\int_{B_{R}}\left|df\right|^{2}-\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}-2n(f-\varphi)\;dV_{f}\\
&+\int_{B_{R}}{\left(\mathrm{scal}_{g}+n(n-1)\right)}{\left(e^{-f}-e^{-\varphi}\right)}\;dV\\
&+\int_{B_{R}}{\left(\mathrm{scal}_{g}+n(n-1)\right)}e^{-\varphi}\;{\left(dV-d\widehat{V}\right)}\\
&+2n\mathcal{B}_{R}{\left(g,\varphi,f\right)}\bigg]+\frac{1}{2n}S_{\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi\right)}\end{split}

(3.6)

The limits of the first two lines and
\mathcal{B}_{R}
are finite as
f-\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}{\left(M\right)}
. Note that
S_{\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi\right)}
is finite by Proposition
3.4
. As for the third line, by Taylor expanding the volume form of
g
in the direction of
h=g-\widehat{g}
, we have the estimate

{\left(\mathrm{scal}_{g}+n(n-1)\right)}{\left(dV-d\widehat{V}\right)}=\frac{1}{2}\left\langle{\left(\mathrm{scal}_{g}+n(n-1)\right)}g,h\right\rangle\;d\widehat{V}+\mathcal{O}{\left(\left|h\right|^{2}\right)}.

This is integrable, since
g\in\mathcal{R}^{2,\alpha}_{\beta}{\left(M,\widehat{g}\right)}
implies
\left|h\right|,\mathrm{scal}_{g}+n(n-1)=\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-\beta r}\right)}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary
3.5
allows us to make the following definition:

Definition 3.7
.

The
renormalised expander entropy
of
{\left(M,g,\varphi\right)}
, relative to a static background metric
\widehat{g}
, is defined as

\mu_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi\right)}:=\inf\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in C^{\infty}{\left(M\right)}\>s.t.\\
f-\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}{\left(M\right)}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi,f\right)}.

In the following, we shall sometimes find it useful to take the infimum over compactly supported functions representing the difference
f-\varphi
. To that end, we define

\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi,\omega\right)}:=\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi,\varphi-2\ln(\omega+1)\right)},

from which it is immediate that

\mu_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi\right)}=\inf\limits_{\omega\in C_{c}^{\infty}{\left(M\right)}}\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi,\omega\right)}.

Should a minimiser
f
of
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}
exist, then
\omega=e^{-\frac{{\left(f-\varphi\right)}}{2}}-1
will be a minimiser of
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}
.

3.2
Existence of a Minimiser on Bounded Domains

We now turn to showing local existence of a minimiser. In aid of that goal, we derive the Euler–Lagrange equation for the entropy.

Proposition 3.8
.

The
Euler–Lagrange equation
for the functional
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi,\cdot\right)}
is

\displaystyle\EL f:=\frac{1}{2n}{\left(2\Delta f+\left|df\right|^{2}+\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}+2nf-2n\varphi-\mathrm{scal}_{g}-n(n-1)-2n\right)}=0.

(3.9)

Proof.

Whether or not (
3.9
) is the Euler–Lagrange equation for
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}
, we may write

\mathcal{W}_{R}(g,\varphi,f)=\frac{1}{n}\int_{B_{R}}\Delta f+\left|df\right|^{2}-n-n\EL f\;dV_{f}.

(3.10)

For a perturbation
f+sv
with
\left|s\right|\ll 1
and
v\in C^{\infty}_{c}{\left(M\right)}
, the first variation of (
3.9
) is

\left.\frac{d}{ds}\right|_{s=0}\EL{\left(f+sv\right)}=\frac{1}{n}{\left(\Delta v+\left\langle df,dv\right\rangle+nv\right)}.

Thus, the integrand of (
3.10
) has first variation

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d}{ds}\right|_{s=0}{\left(\Delta(f+sv)+\left|d(f+sv)\right|^{2}-n-n\EL{\left(f+sv\right)}\right)}=\left\langle df,dv\right\rangle-nv.

As the weighted volume satisfies
\left.\frac{d}{ds}\right|_{s=0}dV_{f+sv}=-v\;dV_{f}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d}{ds}\right|_{s=0}\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi,f+sv\right)}={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\lim\limits_{R\rightarrow\infty}\int_{B_{R}}\left\langle df,dv\right\rangle-nv-v{\left(\Delta f+\left|df\right|^{2}-n-n\EL f\right)}\;dV_{f}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{R\rightarrow\infty}\left[\int_{B_{R}}v\EL f\;dV_{f}-\frac{1}{n}\int_{B_{R}}v\Delta f+v\left|df\right|^{2}-\left\langle df,dv\right\rangle\;dV_{f}\right]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{R\rightarrow\infty}\left[\int_{B_{R}}v\EL f\;dV_{f}+\frac{1}{n}\int_{\partial B_{R}}v\>\nu{\left(f\right)}\;dA_{f}\right].

The limit of the boundary integral above vanishes, as
v
has compact support. The variation of
\mathcal{B}_{R}{\left(g,\varphi,f\right)}
vanishes for the same reason, while the variation of the weighted ADM-mass vanishes entirely, as it does not depend on
f
. All together, this yields the claimed Euler–Lagrange equation.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

Suppose
{\left(M,\widehat{g},\widehat{\varphi}\right)}
is a static triple with scalar curvature
\mathrm{scal}_{\widehat{g}}=-n(n-1)
. Then the Euler-Lagrange equation simplifies to

\EL f=\frac{1}{n}{\left(\Delta f-\Delta\widehat{\varphi}\right)}+\frac{1}{2n}{\left(\left|df\right|^{2}-\left|d\widehat{\varphi}\right|^{2}\right)}+f-\widehat{\varphi}=0.

It is plain to see that
f=\widehat{\varphi}
is a solution of this equation.

We also have the following uniqueness result when
f
and
\varphi
agree at infinity:

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
{\left(M,g,\varphi\right)}
be an AH manifold with approximate potential
\varphi
. Then there exists at most one solution
f
of (
3.9
) such that
f-\varphi\rightarrow 0
as
r\rightarrow\infty
. Moreover, for every bounded domain
\Omega\subset M
, there is at most one function
f
solving
(
3.9
)
on
\Omega
, such that
\left.{\left(f-\varphi\right)}\right|_{\partial\Omega}=0
.

Proof.

Assume
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are two solutions to (
3.9
) and
f_{i}-\varphi\rightarrow 0
as
r\rightarrow\infty
for
i=1,2
. Then, setting
\widetilde{f}:=f_{1}-f_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\Delta\widetilde{f}+\frac{1}{2n}{\left(\left|df_{1}\right|^{2}-\left|df_{2}\right|^{2}\right)}+\widetilde{f}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\Delta\widetilde{f}+\frac{1}{2n}{\left(|d\widetilde{f}|^{2}+2\left\langle d{\left(f_{1}+f_{2}\right)},d\widetilde{f}\right\rangle\right)}+\widetilde{f}.

(3.13)

Since
f_{i}-\varphi\rightarrow 0
as
r\rightarrow\infty
for
i=1,2
we have

\widetilde{f}={\left(f_{1}-\varphi\right)}+{\left(\varphi-f_{2}\right)}\rightarrow 0

as
r\rightarrow\infty
. Therefore, either
\widetilde{f}\equiv 0
or
\widetilde{f}
achieves an extremum on
M
. In the former case we are done.
In the latter case,
\widetilde{f}
has either a positive maximum or a negative minimum.
If
\widetilde{f}
has a positive maximum, let
p:=\mathrm{argmax}_{M}\widetilde{f}
. Then
\widetilde{f}{\left(p\right)}>0
,
d\widetilde{f}{\left(p\right)}=0
, and
\Delta\widetilde{f}{\left(p\right)}\geq 0
, which contradicts (
3.13
). The case of a negative minimum is treated analogously, which completes the proof
The proof for bounded domains is the same after replacing instances of
M
by
\Omega
.
∎

Next, we have a result which generalises Remark
3.11
:

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
{\left(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times M,\mathfrak{g}\right)}
be a
(n+1)
-dimensional AH manifold, such that

\mathfrak{g}=e^{-2\varphi}\;d\tau^{2}+g

and

\mathrm{scal}_{\mathfrak{g}}=-n(n+1).

If
f
solves (
3.9
) with respect to
g
and
\varphi
, and if
f-\varphi\rightarrow 0
as
r\rightarrow\infty
, then
f=\varphi
.

Proof.

By direct computation (see Section
2.3
), one finds that

\mathrm{scal}_{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathrm{scal}_{g}-2\Delta_{g}\varphi-2\left|d\varphi\right|_{g}^{2}.

Inserting
\mathrm{scal}_{g}=-n(n+1)+2\Delta_{g}\varphi+2\left|d\varphi\right|_{g}^{2}
into the Euler–Lagrange equation (
3.9
), we obtain

\EL f=\frac{1}{2n}{\left(2\Delta_{g}f-2\Delta_{g}\varphi+\left|df\right|^{2}_{g}-\left|d\varphi\right|_{g}^{2}\right)}+f-\varphi=0.

Clearly
\EL\varphi=0
, and by Proposition
3.12
we have
f=\varphi
.
∎

Before proving local existence of a minimiser for the entropy, we will prove two-sided
H^{1}_{\varphi}
-bounds for the entropy. The following Poincaré type inequality will be of aid in this.

Lemma 3.15
.

Suppose
\varphi
satisfies (
2.23
) and let
\omega=\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-\beta r}\right)}
, with
\frac{n}{2}<\beta<n
. For every
0<\Lambda<\frac{2n-1}{4}
, we can find a compact subset
K\subset M
such that

\int_{M}\left|\nabla\omega\right|^{2}\;dV_{\varphi}\geq\int_{K}{\left(\frac{1}{2}\Delta\varphi+\frac{1}{4}\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}\right)}\omega^{2}\;dV_{\varphi}+\int_{M\setminus K}\Lambda\omega^{2}\;dV_{\varphi}

Proof.

Set
V:=e^{\frac{\varphi}{2}}
. Then

\begin{split}0\leq{}&\int_{M}\left|\nabla{\left(V^{-1}\omega\right)}\right|^{2}\;dV=\int_{M}\left|V^{-1}\nabla\omega-V^{-2}\omega\nabla V\right|^{2}\;dV\\
={}&\int_{M}V^{-2}\left|\nabla\omega\right|^{2}-V^{-3}\left\langle\nabla\omega^{2},\nabla V\right\rangle+V^{-4}\omega^{2}\left|\nabla V\right|^{2}\;dV\\
={}&\int_{M}V^{-2}\left|\nabla\omega\right|^{2}-\omega^{2}{\left(V^{-3}\Delta V+2V^{-4}\left|\nabla V\right|^{2}\right)}+\delta{\left(\omega^{2}V^{-3}\nabla V\right)}\;dV\end{split}

(3.16)

Since
\omega=\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-\beta r}\right)}
by assumption, we must have
\omega^{2}=\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-(n+\varepsilon)r}\right)}
for some
\varepsilon>0
. In particular,

\int_{M}\delta{\left(\omega^{2}V^{-3}\nabla V\right)}\;dV=-\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial B_{R}}\omega^{2}\nu(\varphi)\;dA_{\varphi}=0.

Recall the asymptotics of
\varphi
, provided by the construction in Lemma
2.22
:

\displaystyle\Delta\varphi+\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=n+\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-\beta r}\right)},

\displaystyle\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=1+\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-r}\right)}

Thus for every
0<\Lambda<\frac{2n-1}{4}
, we can choose a compact set
K\subset M
such that on the complement
M\setminus K
we have

V^{-1}\Delta V+2V^{-2}\left|\nabla V\right|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}{\left(\Delta\varphi+\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}\right)}-\frac{1}{4}\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}\geq\Lambda.

Using this and rearranging (
3.16
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{M}V^{-2}\left|\nabla\omega\right|^{2}\;dV

\displaystyle\geq\int_{M}V^{-2}\omega^{2}{\left(V^{-1}\Delta V+2V^{-2}\left|\nabla V\right|^{2}\right)}\;dV

\displaystyle\geq\int_{K}V^{-2}\omega^{2}{\left(V^{-1}\Delta V+2V^{-2}\left|\nabla V\right|^{2}\right)}\;dV+\int_{M\setminus K}\Lambda V^{-2}\omega^{2}\;dV,

as desired.
∎

Now for the two-sided
H^{1}_{\varphi}
-bounds.

Proposition 3.17
.

Suppose
g\in\mathcal{R}^{2,\alpha}_{\beta}{\left(M,\widehat{g}\right)}
for some
\frac{n}{2}<\beta<n
, and that
\varphi
satisfies (
2.23
). Then there are constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}>0
depending only on
g,\widehat{g},\varphi
, and a constant
C_{\varepsilon}>0
depending on the previous parameters as well as on
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
, such that

C_{1}\left|\left|\omega\right|\right|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varphi}}-C_{2}\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi,\omega\right)}\leq C_{\varepsilon}{\left(1+\left|\left|\omega\right|\right|_{H^{1}_{\varphi}}^{\varepsilon}\right)}\left|\left|\omega\right|\right|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varphi}}+C_{3},

for every
\omega\in H^{1}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}
.

Proof.

Setting
f=\varphi-2\ln{\left(1+\omega\right)}
in the decomposition (
3.6
), we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}(g,\varphi,\omega)={}&\frac{1}{2n}\lim_{R\to\infty}\left[\int_{B_{R}}4\left|d\omega\right|^{2}-4(\omega+1)\left\langle d\omega,d\varphi\right\rangle+4n{\left(1+\omega\right)}^{2}\ln{\left(1+\omega\right)}\right.\\
&\qquad\qquad+{\left(\omega^{2}+2\omega\right)}{\left(\mathrm{scal}_{g}+n(n-1)\right)}\;dV_{\varphi}+2n\mathcal{B}_{R}{\left(g,\varphi,f\right)}\biggr]+C(g,\varphi),\end{split}

(3.18)

where

C(g,\varphi):=\frac{1}{2n}\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{B_{R}}{\left(\mathrm{scal}_{g}+n(n-1)\right)}e^{-\varphi}{\left(dV-d\widehat{V}\right)}+\frac{1}{2n}S_{\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi\right)}.

This was shown to be finite during the proof of Corollary
3.5
. Since
\omega\in H^{1}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}
, we must have

\lim_{R\to\infty}\mathcal{B}_{R}(g,\varphi,f)=\frac{1}{n}\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{\partial B_{R}}2\ln{\left(1+\omega\right)}\nu{\left(\varphi\right)}\;dA_{\varphi}=\frac{1}{n}\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{\partial B_{R}}2\omega\>\nu{\left(\varphi\right)}\;dA_{\varphi},

as the higher order terms of
\ln(1+\omega)=\omega+\mathcal{O}{\left(\omega^{2}\right)}
do not contribute to the limit. By the same logic, we may exchange
2n\mathcal{B}_{R}{\left(g,\varphi,f\right)}
for

\int_{\partial B_{R}}2{\left(\omega^{2}+2\omega\right)}\nu{\left(\varphi\right)}\;dA_{\varphi}=\int_{B_{R}}4{\left(\omega+1\right)}\left\langle d\omega,d\varphi\right\rangle-2{\left(\omega^{2}+2\omega\right)}{\left(\Delta\varphi+\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}\right)}\;dV_{\varphi}

in (
3.18
). After the immediate cancellations, we are left with

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}(g,\varphi,\omega)={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2n}\lim_{R\to\infty}\left[\int_{B_{R}}4\left|d\omega\right|^{2}+4n{\left(1+\omega\right)}^{2}\ln{\left(1+\omega\right)}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+{\left(\omega^{2}+2\omega\right)}{\left(\mathrm{scal}_{g}+n(n-1)-2\Delta\varphi-2\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}\right)}\;dV_{\varphi}\biggr]+C(g,\varphi).

By defining the quantities

\displaystyle H{\left(\omega\right)}

\displaystyle:=2n{\left(\omega+1\right)}^{2}\ln{\left({\left(\omega+1\right)}^{2}\right)}-2n{\left(\omega^{2}+2\omega\right)},

and

R{\left(g,\varphi\right)}:=\mathrm{scal}_{g}+n{\left(n-1\right)}-2{\left(\Delta\varphi+\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}-n\right)},

we may simply write

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}(g,\varphi,\omega)={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2n}\lim_{R\to\infty}\left[\int_{B_{R}}4\left|d\omega\right|^{2}+H(\omega)+{\left(\omega^{2}+2\omega\right)}R(g,\varphi)\;dV_{\varphi}\right]+C(g,\varphi).

Note that
R(g,\varphi)=\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-\beta r}\right)}
by assumption, while
H
satisfies

0\leq H{\left(\omega\right)}\leq C_{\varepsilon}{\left(\omega^{2}+\omega^{2+\varepsilon}\right)},

for arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
. The upper bound for the entropy is now derived using Young’s Inequality:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi,\omega\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n}\lim\limits_{R\rightarrow\infty}\bigg[\int_{B_{R}}4\left|d\omega\right|^{2}+H{\left(\omega\right)}+{\left(\omega^{2}+2\omega\right)}R{\left(g,\varphi\right)}\;dV_{\varphi}\bigg]+C(g,\varphi)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2n}\lim\limits_{R\rightarrow\infty}\bigg[\int_{B_{R}}4\left|d\omega\right|^{2}+C_{n}\omega^{2}+C_{\varepsilon}\omega^{2+\varepsilon}+4\left|R{\left(g,\varphi\right)}\right|^{2}dV_{\varphi}\bigg]+C(g,\varphi)

\displaystyle\leq\widehat{C}_{\varepsilon}{\left(1+\left|\left|\omega\right|\right|^{\varepsilon}_{H^{1}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}}\right)}\left|\left|\omega\right|\right|^{2}_{H^{1}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}}+\left|\left|R{\left(g,\varphi\right)}\right|\right|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}}+C(g,\varphi),

where
\widehat{C}_{\varepsilon}>0
is a constant depending on
\varepsilon,g,\widehat{g},\varphi
. Due to its assumed decay, the
L^{2}_{\varphi}
-norm of
R{\left(g,\varphi\right)}
is finite. Note we have also used the Sobolev embedding
H^{1}_{\varphi}\subset L^{2+\varepsilon}_{\varphi}
for
\varepsilon
small enough (see
[
Lee
, Lemma 3.6(c)]
).

To find the claimed lower bound, we choose a compact subset
K\subset M
such that Lemma
3.15
holds for some fixed
0<\Lambda<\frac{2n-1}{4}
. By possibly enlarging
K
, we may also assume that, on
M\setminus K
, we have

\Lambda+R(g,\varphi)-\varepsilon>0

Applying Young’s inequality and Lemma
3.15
, we obtain

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{R\rightarrow\infty}\int_{B_{R}}

\displaystyle 4\left|d\omega\right|^{2}+H{\left(\omega\right)}+R{\left(g,\varphi\right)}{\left(\omega^{2}+2\omega\right)}dV_{\varphi}

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{R\rightarrow\infty}\int_{B_{R}}4\left|d\omega\right|^{2}+H(\omega)+{\left(R(g,\varphi)-\varepsilon\right)}\omega^{2}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left|R(g,\varphi)\right|^{2}\;dV_{\varphi}

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\int_{K}2\left|d\omega\right|^{2}+H{\left(\omega\right)}+{\left(R{\left(g,\varphi\right)}-\varepsilon+\Delta\varphi+\frac{1}{2}\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}\right)}\omega^{2}\;dV_{\varphi}

\displaystyle+\lim\limits_{R\rightarrow\infty}\int_{B_{R}\backslash K}2\left|d\omega\right|^{2}+H{\left(\omega\right)}+{\left(R{\left(g,\varphi\right)}-\varepsilon+2\Lambda\right)}\omega^{2}\;dV_{\varphi}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left|\left|R{\left(g,\varphi\right)}\right|\right|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}}

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{R\rightarrow\infty}\int_{B_{R}}2\left|d\omega\right|^{2}+\Lambda\omega^{2}\;dV_{\varphi}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left|\left|R{\left(g,\varphi\right)}\right|\right|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}}

\displaystyle+\int_{K}H(\omega)+{\left(R(g,\varphi)-\varepsilon-\Lambda+\Delta\varphi+\frac{1}{2}\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}\right)}\omega^{2}\;dV_{\varphi}.

Now, since
g
and
\varphi
are
C^{\ell,\alpha}
(and
\ell\geq 2
), we can use the definition of
K
, as well as
H{\left(\omega\right)}\geq 0
, and that it has faster than quadratic growth, to deduce that

H(\omega)+{\left(R(g,\varphi)-\varepsilon-\Lambda+\Delta\varphi+\frac{1}{2}\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}\right)}\omega^{2}\geq-C_{2}

on
K
. Here
C_{2}>0
is a constant depending only on
g,\widehat{g},\varphi
. Putting everything together yields the claimed lower bound:

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{R\rightarrow\infty}\int_{B_{R}}4\left|d\omega\right|^{2}+H{\left(\omega\right)}+R{\left(g,\varphi\right)}{\left(\omega^{2}+2\omega\right)}dV_{\varphi}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\geq C_{1}\left|\left|\omega\right|\right|_{H^{1}_{\varphi}}^{2}-C_{2}\Vol_{\varphi}\!{\left(K\right)}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left|\left|R(g,\varphi)\right|\right|_{L^{2}_{\varphi}}^{2}.

∎

Note that, by a density argument, Proposition
3.17
will allow us to use the alternative definition of the entropy:

\mu_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi\right)}:=\inf\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in C^{\infty}{\left(M\right)}\;s.t.\\
f-\varphi\in H^{1}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi,f\right)}=\inf_{\omega\in H^{1}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}}\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi,\omega\right)}.

We can now prove existence of a minimiser on bounded domains by a standard variational argument. For this, it will be convenient to consider a local version of the entropy. For a bounded domain
\Omega\subset M
, we define
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g},\Omega}
by replacing integration over
M
with integration over
\Omega
in Definition
3.3
. We define
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g},\Omega}
analogously. Finally, we define the local entropy:

\mu_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g},\Omega}{\left(g,\varphi\right)}:=\inf\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in C^{\infty}{\left(\Omega\right)}\;s.t.\\
f-\varphi\in H^{1}_{\varphi,0}{\left(\Omega\right)}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g},\Omega}{\left(g,\varphi,f\right)}=\inf_{\omega\in H^{1}_{\varphi,0}{\left(\Omega\right)}}\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g},\Omega}{\left(g,\varphi,\omega\right)}

where
H^{1}_{\varphi,0}{\left(\Omega\right)}:=\left\{\psi\in H^{1}_{\varphi}(\Omega)\;:\;\psi|_{\partial\Omega}=0\right\}
.

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
\Omega\subset M
be a bounded domain with smooth boundary. Then there is a function
\omega\in H^{1}_{\varphi,0}{\left(\Omega\right)}
which realises the infimum of
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g},\Omega}{\left(g,\varphi,\cdot\right)}
.

This function
\omega
is the unique such minimiser vanishing on the boundary. Equivalently, there is a unique
f\in C^{\ell,\alpha}{\left(\Omega\right)}
solving (
3.9
) on
\Omega
, such that
\left.{\left(f-\varphi\right)}\right|_{\partial\Omega}=0
, and it achieves the infimum of
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g},\Omega}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.17
, we know that

\mu_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g},\Omega}{\left(g,\varphi\right)}=\inf_{\omega\in H^{1}_{\varphi,0}{\left(M\right)}}\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g},\Omega}{\left(g,\varphi,\omega\right)}>-\infty.

Let
\omega_{i}\in H^{1}_{\varphi,0}{\left(\Omega\right)}
be a minimising sequence for
\mu_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g},\Omega}{\left(g,\varphi\right)}
.

As the weighted volume measure
e^{-\varphi}dV_{g}
is equivalent to the unweighted
dV_{g}
on bounded domains, we can apply the Rellich–Kondrachov theorem to deduce the existence of a subsequence, still denoted
\omega_{i}
, such that
\omega_{i}\rightharpoonup\omega
in
H^{1}_{\varphi}{\left(\Omega\right)}
and
\omega_{i}\rightarrow\omega
in
L_{\varphi}^{p}{\left(\Omega\right)}
for some fixed
p<\frac{2n}{n-2}
.

As the functional
\omega\mapsto\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g},\Omega}{\left(g,\varphi,\omega\right)}
is coercive and convex with respect to
d\omega
, it is lower-semicontinuous on
H^{1}_{\varphi,0}{\left(\Omega\right)}
. Hence

\mu_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g},\Omega}{\left(g,\varphi\right)}\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g},\Omega}{\left(g,\varphi,\omega\right)}\leq\liminf\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g},\Omega}{\left(g,\varphi,\omega_{i}\right)}\leq\mu_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g},\Omega}{\left(g,\varphi\right)}.

Therefore,
\omega\in H^{1}_{\varphi,0}{\left(\Omega\right)}
is the desired minimiser. It follows that
f:=\varphi-2\ln{\left(\omega+1\right)}
is a weak solution to the Euler–Lagrange equation (
3.9
). We may thus use standard arguments, involving elliptic regularity theory and the Sobolev embedding theorem, to deduce that
f\in C^{\ell,\alpha}{\left(\Omega\right)}
. Moreover,
f
is the unique such solution due to Proposition
3.12
.
∎

3.3
Existence of a Global Minimiser

We now turn to proving the existence of a global minimiser for the renormalised expander entropy. This roughly follows the procedure in Section
5
of
[
DKM
]
.

First of all, we find appropriate upper and lower barriers. For this, it will be convenient to rewrite the Euler–Lagrange equation (
3.9
) as follows:

\displaystyle 0=\EL f

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n}{\left(2\Delta f+\left|df\right|^{2}+\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}+2nf-2n\varphi-\mathrm{scal}_{g}-n(n-1)-2n\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}{\left({\left(\Delta+n\right)}{\left(f-\varphi\right)}+\frac{1}{2}{\left(\left|df\right|^{2}-\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}\right)}+{\left(\Delta\varphi+\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}-n\right)}-\frac{1}{2}{\left(\mathrm{scal}_{g}+n(n-1)\right)}\right)}.

The second line follows from adding and subtracting
\frac{1}{n}\Delta\varphi+\frac{1}{n}\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}
. Now, if
f
is a minimiser we have
\EL f=0
, hence, setting
u:=f-\varphi
,

\Delta u+\frac{1}{2}\left|du\right|^{2}+\left<d\varphi,du\right>+nu=-{\left(\Delta\varphi+\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}-n\right)}+\frac{1}{2}{\left(\mathrm{scal}_{g}+n(n-1)\right)}.

(3.20)

Proposition 3.21
.

Let
{\left(M,g,\varphi\right)}
be an asymptotically hyperbolic manifold with approximate potential
\varphi
, and consider
\Omega\subset M
a bounded domain with smooth boundary. Let
u=f-\varphi
be a solution of (
3.20
) with
\left.u\right|_{\partial\Omega}=0
. Then we have

\inf_{\Omega}\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{scal}_{g}+\frac{n\left(n-1\right)}{2}-\Delta\varphi-\left|d\varphi\right|+n\right)\leq nu\leq\sup_{\Omega}\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{scal}_{g}+\frac{n\left(n-1\right)}{2}-\Delta\varphi-\left|d\varphi\right|+n\right).

Proof.

If we evaluate (
3.20
) at a maximum point
p:=\mathrm{arg\,max}_{\Omega}u
then, since
\nabla u\left(p\right)=0
and
\Delta u\left(p\right)\geq 0
, we see that

n\cdot\sup_{\Omega}u\leq\sup_{\Omega}\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{scal}_{g}+\frac{n\left(n-1\right)}{2}-\Delta\varphi-\left|d\varphi\right|+n\right).

If instead
p:=\argmin_{\Omega}u
, then
\nabla u\left(p\right)=0
and
\Delta u\left(p\right)\leq 0
, which yields

n\cdot\inf_{\Omega}u\geq\inf_{\Omega}\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{scal}_{g}+\frac{n\left(n-1\right)}{2}-\Delta\varphi-\left|d\varphi\right|+n\right).

∎

Lemma 3.22
.

Suppose
g
is AH with approximate potential
\varphi
and
\left|\mathrm{scal}_{g}+n(n-1)\right|=\mathcal{O}(e^{-\beta r})
. Then there exists an
L^{2}_{\varphi}
-integrable function
b
, with the property that the Dirichlet solution of

\Delta u+nu+\frac{1}{2}\left|du\right|^{2}+\left\langle d\varphi,du\right\rangle=\frac{1}{2}R(g,\varphi)

(3.23)

on any bounded set
\Omega\subset M
, satisfies
\left|u\right|\leq b
.
As in the preceding section,
R{\left(g,\varphi\right)}=\mathrm{scal}_{g}+n(n-1)-2{\left(\Delta\varphi+\left|d\varphi\right|^{2}-n\right)}
.

Proof.

Let
K\subset M
be a compact set, on whose complement we can write
g
as in the proof of Lemma
2.22
. Consider the radial function
b(r)=\lambda e^{-\beta r}
, for some
\lambda>0
still to be determined. Then

\displaystyle\Delta b+nb+\frac{1}{2}\left|db\right|^{2}+\left\langle d\varphi,db\right\rangle={}

\displaystyle-b^{\prime\prime}-(n+G-\psi^{\prime})b^{\prime}+nb+\frac{1}{2}(b^{\prime})^{2}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle{\left(n+n\beta-\beta^{2}\right)}\lambda e^{-\beta r}+{\left(G-\psi^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}\beta\lambda e^{-\beta r}\right)}\beta\lambda e^{-\beta r}

where
G
and
\psi^{\prime}=\partial_{r}\psi
are as in the aforementioned lemma. Note that the factor
n+n\beta-\beta^{2}>0
for
\beta
in the given range. As
\left|G-\psi^{\prime}\right|=\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-r}\right)}
and
\left|R(g,\varphi)\right|=\mathcal{O}{\left(e^{-\beta r}\right)}
, it is possible to find constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
, and a radius
\rho\gg 1
such that

\left|G-\psi^{\prime}\right|\leq C_{1}e^{-r}\qquad\&\qquad\left|R(g,\varphi)\right|\leq C_{2}e^{-\beta r}

for
r\geq\rho
. We can then choose
\lambda
such that, when
\rho
is sufficiently large, we have

{\left(n+n\beta-\beta^{2}\right)}\lambda\geq\frac{1}{2}{\left(\beta\lambda\right)}^{2}e^{-\beta\rho}+C_{1}\beta\lambda e^{-\rho}+\frac{1}{2}C_{2}.

Consequently,
b(r)=\lambda e^{-\beta r}
is a supersolution

\Delta b+nb+\frac{1}{2}\left|db\right|^{2}+\left\langle d\varphi,db\right\rangle\geq\frac{1}{2}R(g,\varphi)

(3.24)

on
M\setminus B_{\rho}
. For simplicity of further arguments, we shall additionally assume that
\lambda
and
\rho
are chosen such that
\lambda e^{-\beta\rho}\geq\sup_{M}\left|R(g,\varphi)\right|
, and extend
b
to all of
B_{\rho}
by the constant value it has on
\partial B_{\rho}
. Let
\Omega\subset M
be an arbitrary bounded domain with smooth boundary, and let
u
be a solution of (
3.23
), with
u|_{\partial\Omega}=0
. By design,
u\leq b
everywhere, except possibly in the interior of
\Omega\setminus B_{\rho}
. Assume this set has an interior, as we are otherwise done, and that the function
u-b
has a positive local maximum at
p\in\Omega\setminus B_{\rho}
:

u(p)-b(p)>0,\qquad du(p)-db(p)=0,\qquad\Delta u(p)-\Delta b(p)\geq 0.

Then, combining (
3.23
) and (
3.24
), we must have

\Delta u(p)-\Delta b{\left(p\right)}+nu(p)-nb(p)\leq 0,

which is a clear contradiction. The conclusion is that
u\leq b
on all of
M
, and by finding
b
before the introduction of either
\Omega
or
u
, it should be clear that it is independent hereof.

The same argument, with inverted inequalities, proves that
\lambda
may be chosen such that
-\lambda e^{-\beta r}\leq u
on all of
M
, thus completing the proof of the lemma.
∎

We can now prove the existence of a global minimiser of the entropy.

Theorem 3.25
.

Let
{\left(\widehat{g},\widehat{\varphi}\right)}
be a static pair, and let
g\in\mathcal{R}^{2,\alpha}_{\beta}{\left(M,\widehat{g}\right)}
be an AH metric with approximate potential
\varphi
, with
\frac{n}{2}<\beta<n
. Then there is a unique
f
such that
f-\varphi\in C^{2,\alpha}_{\beta}{\left(M\right)}\cap C^{\ell,\alpha}{\left(M\right)}
and

\mu_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi\right)}=\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{AH},\widehat{g}}{\left(g,\varphi,f\right)}.

Furthermore, if
g\in\mathcal{R}_{\beta}^{\ell,\alpha}{\left(M,\widehat{g}\right)}
(or
g\in\mathcal{R}^{\ell}_{\varphi}{\left(M,\widehat{g}\right)}
) for some
\ell\geq 2
, then
f-\varphi\in C^{\ell,\alpha}_{\beta}{\left(M\right)}
(respectively
H^{\ell}_{\varphi}{\left(M\right)}
).

Proof.

Let
\left\{\Omega_{i}\right\}^{\infty}_{i=1}
be a sequence of bounded domains with smooth boundary, such that
\Omega_{i}\subset\Omega_{i+1}
and
\bigcup^{\infty}_{i=1}\Omega_{i}=M
. Denote by
u_{i}=f_{i}-\varphi
a sequence of solutions to (
3.23
) such that
u_{i}\in C^{\ell,\alpha}{\left(\Omega_{i}\right)}\cap C^{0}{\left(\overline{\Omega_{i}}\right)}
and
\left.u_{i}\right|_{\partial\Omega_{i}}=0
. According to Proposition
3.21
,
